Anti-Concentration Inequalities for the Difference of Maxima of Gaussian Random Vectors
Alexandre Bellonilabel=e1]abn5@duke.edu, Ethan X. Fanglabel=e2]ethan.fang@duke.edu, Shuting Shenlabel=e3]ss1446@duke.edu

Abstract
We derive novel anti-concentration bounds for the difference between the maximal values of two Gaussian random vectors across various settings. Our bounds are dimension-free, scaling with the dimension of the Gaussian vectors only through the smaller expected maximum of the Gaussian subvectors. In addition, our bounds hold under the degenerate covariance structures, which previous results do not cover. In addition, we show that our conditions are sharp under the homogeneous component-wise variance setting, while we only impose some mild assumptions on the covariance structures under the heterogeneous variance setting. We apply the new anti-concentration bounds to derive the central limit theorem for the maximizers of discrete empirical processes. Finally, we back up our theoretical findings with comprehensive numerical studies.

1
Introduction

The concentration patterns of random variables are pivotal in empirical process theory, and find applications to different communities such as statistics
[
6
]
, probability
[
9
]
, and discrete mathematics
[
28
]
. For example, in high-dimensional statistical analysis, the celebrated work
[
7
]
derives the anti-concentration bounds of the maximum of Gaussian random vectors to establish central limit theorems for
the maximum of a sum of high-dimensional random vectors. Such results provide the foundation for constructing confidence regions through Gaussian and other bootstrap approximations under the high-dimensional setting, which find many important applications
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
15
,
2
]
. This motivated several other works to generalize the results under different settings
[
15
,
18
,
12
,
14
,
31
,
19
]
.

Our focus is to study the anti-concentration bounds that characterize how fast different statistics concentrate,
and we rely on the Lévy concentration function
[
35
]
to characterize the concentration, which is defined as

\mathcal{L}(Y,\varepsilon):=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}(|Y-t|\leq\varepsilon),

where
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and
Y
is the random variable of interest.
For example, suppose that
Y
is the maximum of a Gaussian random vector.
[
10
]
shows that
\mathcal{L}(Y,\varepsilon)\leq 2\varepsilon(\sqrt{2\log p}+2)/\underline{\sigma}
, where

\underline{\sigma}^{2}
is the minimal component-wise variance of the Gaussian random vector.
Other works derive sharper bounds for such anti-concentration results in different forms. Specifically,
[
9
,
27
,
17
]
derive dimension-free bounds that depend on the expectation or the variance of the maximum component of the Gaussian random vector, allowing for generalization of the results to infinite-dimensional scenarios.

We establish new anti-concentration bounds for the difference of the maxima of two Gaussian random vectors. Let
X=(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a Gaussian random vector with marginal means

\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
and variances
\{\sigma^{2}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
,
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
be a nontrivial partition of
[p]=\{1,\ldots,p\}
such that both
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are nonempty,
and
(M_{\mathcal{A}},M_{\mathcal{B}})=(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i},\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j})
. We aim to bound the Lévy concentration function of
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
, namely:

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}(|M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}-t|\leq\varepsilon),\quad\text{for any }\varepsilon>0.

(1.1)

This difference in maxima appears naturally when we are interested in the maximizers of some empirical processes
[
21
]
. We also note that (
1.1
) plays a crucial role in establishing the validity of bootstrap approximations for the distribution of the maxima difference of sums of (non-Gaussian) random vectors (see Section
3
).

To establish the anti-concentration bounds, we bound the density function of the difference of the maxima of two Gaussian random vectors.
Under the assumption that the minimum eigenvalue of the covariance matrix of
X
is strictly positive,
[
21
]
develops a conditional anti-concentration inequality, where they bound the density by conditioning on one of the Gaussian random vectors and applying the anti-concentration bound for a single Gaussian maximum in
[
10
]
to the other Gaussian random vector. Thus, the bound scales with the minimal eigenvalue of the full covariance matrix.
Although the minimum eigenvalue is bounded away from zero in many settings, as we will discuss later, this assumption does not hold in some applications of our interest. Our result does not require such a minimal eigenvalue assumption. Indeed, even if the covariance matrix has zero eigenvalues,
the Lévy concentration function remains well-behaved.
1
1

1

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
be i.i.d.
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
random variables and define
X_{1}=\xi_{1}
,
X_{2}=\xi_{2}
,
X_{3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})
and
X_{4}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})
. Then
\sigma_{i}^{2}=1,i=1,\ldots,4
and
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}])=0
, while
{\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}}\ \mathbb{P}\big(|\max\{X_{3},X_{4}\}-\max\{X_{1},X_{2}\}-t|\leq\varepsilon\big)\leq{(8/\sqrt{\pi})}\varepsilon
.
One such setting of interest is when
X_{\mathcal{A}}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}Z
and
X_{\mathcal{B}}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}Z
, where
Z
is a
d
-dimensional Gaussian random vector and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}},\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
are two
p\times d
matrices with
p>d
. In this case, the minimal eigenvalue assumption is violated, rendering prior results inapplicable. In comparison, our results still hold, which sheds new insights into anti-concentration patterns.

1.1
Major contributions

Our primary theoretical contribution is to establish new anti-concentration bounds for the difference between the maxima of two Gaussian vectors.
A significant challenge in this context is the non-convexity of the function that maps a vector to the difference of the maximum values in two parts of the vector,
rendering existing techniques for establishing anti-concentration bounds inapplicable. To address this, we develop new proof strategies. Central to our approach is a new smoothing technique, where we transform the density into disjoint integrals over centered Gaussian variables, and then utilize the derivative of a conditional probability for Gaussian random vectors as an intermediate bound to achieve the final anti-concentration bound.

Our new anti-concentration bounds hold under generic conditions. The bound is dimension-free and depends on the minimum of the expectation of the two Gaussian maxima.
Indeed, if one of the maxima concentrates fast enough due to the much higher subset dimension, this maximum behaves essentially like a constant, and the anti-concentration is governed by the variation in the other maximum.
Under the condition that all components have equal variances, i.e.,
\sigma_{i}=\sigma
for all
i\in[p]
, our conditions are broadly applicable. In particular, we have that

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq C\cdot\frac{\varepsilon/{\sigma}}{1-\bar{\rho}}\cdot\min\big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|{/\sigma}\big),\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma\big)\big\},

(1.2)

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
p
, and
\bar{\rho}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})
is the highest correlation across the subsets.
In the case of unequal marginal variances, our bound scales with the reciprocal of
\min_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{ij}/\sigma_{i}
or
\min_{j\in\mathcal{B},i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{ij}/\sigma_{j}
(see Theorem
2.4
for details). This term is similar to the minimal marginal standard deviation in the anti-concentration bounds for the single Gaussian maximum
[
9
]
.
For the case where
\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=1
for some
i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}
, a bound in the form of (
1.2
) cannot hold. Instead, we show that the Lévy concentration function satisfies
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq C_{X}\varepsilon+\zeta_{X}
, where
C_{X}
is the concentration rate that depends on the covariance structure of
X
, and
\zeta_{X}
is the residual probability of
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
being the maxima, which is negligible in many settings (see Corollary
2.2
and Proposition
2.3
).

Combining our new anti-concentration bounds with the Gaussian approximation techniques in
[
12
]
, we derive a new central limit theorem (CLT) and bootstrap approximation for the distribution of the maximizers of empirical processes on a discrete domain.
Specifically, consider a normalized empirical process
Y=\{Y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p}
with each component
Y_{j}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\xi_{ij}+a_{j})
standardized to unit variance, where
\xi_{ij}
are independent centered random variables, and
a_{j}
’s are deterministic constants. For a subset
\mathcal{A}\subsetneq[p]
, the probability that
\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}Y_{j}
is a subset of
\mathcal{A}
is characterized by the probability of a difference of maxima being positive:

\mathbb{P}(\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}Y_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A})=\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}Y_{i}-\max_{j\in[p]\setminus\mathcal{A}}Y_{j}>0).

By using a maximizer of a Gaussian process
X=(X_{j})_{j=1}^{p}
mirroring
Y
’s mean and covariance structure,
we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}Y_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A})-\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}X_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A})\big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}Y_{i}-\max_{j\in[p]\setminus\mathcal{A}}Y_{j}>0)-\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}-\max_{j\in[p]\setminus\mathcal{A}}X_{j}>0)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{1-\bar{\rho}}\cdot\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big),\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in[p]\setminus\mathcal{A}}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|\big)\Big\}\left(\frac{\log^{3}(pn)}{n}\right)^{1/4},

where
\bar{\rho}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in[p]\backslash\mathcal{A}}\operatorname{Corr}(Y_{i},Y_{j})
is the highest correlation between the components in different subsets.

1.2
Literature review

Studies of anti-concentration inequalities for maximal statistics predominantly focus on the distribution of continuous random variables, particularly those related to functions of Gaussian random variables
[
4
,
37
,
25
,
45
,
20
,
44
,
9
]
.
[
9
]
sets a precedent by bounding the Lévy concentration function of the maximum of centered Gaussian processes.
[
9
]
utilizes the monotonicity of a component of the density function to establish a dimension-free bound, which scales inversely with the minimal component-wise standard deviation. An alternative approach for non-centered Gaussian processes appears in
[
10
]
, where Nazarov’s inequality is used to bound the concentration at the intersection boundaries of half-spaces.

[
15
]
advances these bounds by replacing the inverse of the minimal standard deviation with the inverse of a function of ordered standard deviations, which can significantly exceed the minimal standard deviation under specific conditions.
[
17
]
introduces a novel proof strategy that relies on the
s
-concavity of the Gaussian measure, providing upper and lower bounds for the anti-concentration of Gaussian order statistics based on the variance. These developments in characterizing anti-concentration patterns have been extensively applied to establish central limit theorems for various statistics, contributing to recent advances in uncertainty quantification of stochastic processes
[
10
,
22
,
21
]
, the improvement of the Berry–Esseen bounds in high dimensions
[
34
,
27
,
32
]
, the bootstrap approximation for regression estimates and random matrices
[
39
,
6
,
43
,
33
]
, and the construction of confidence bands in non-parametric settings
[
8
,
23
,
24
]
.

Anti-concentration inequalities are also of interest in combinatorial applications such as random graphs. For example,
[
16
]
investigates the anti-concentration of polynomials of Bernoulli random variables within a combinatorial context to study subgraph counts in random graphs.
[
28
]
explores anti-concentration bounds for edge statistics in
C
-Ramsey graphs, focusing on the number of edges within random vertex subsets of such graphs. Additionally,
[
41
]
offers precise estimates for the Littlewood-Offord problem
[
30
]
by characterizing the anti-concentration of weighted sums of independent random variables with bounded variance and limited third moments.
[
13
,
40
,
36
]
further extend the Littlewood-Offord problem to higher-degree polynomials.

Recall that, except for
[
21
]
, all existing works do not apply to the difference of maximum values of two Gaussian random vectors. Compared to
[
21
]
, our bounds rely on (pairwise) correlations rather than the minimal eigenvalue of the covariance matrix.

Paper organization.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
presents the main theoretical results on the anti-concentration inequalities under homogeneous and heterogeneous variance conditions, respectively. Section
3
applies the anti-concentration results to establish the validity of bootstrap approximations for estimating the distribution of maximizers of empirical processes. Section
4
conducts numerical simulations to evaluate the anti-concentration bounds under various settings. The proofs for the theoretical results are provided in Section
5
, followed by concluding remarks in Section
6
.

Notations.

For real numbers
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
x\vee y:=\max(x,y)
and
x\wedge y:=\min(x,y)
, and define
(x)_{+}=x\vee 0
. For an integer
p
, denote by
[p]
the index set
\{1,\ldots,p\}
. For a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, denote by
\|v\|_{2}=(\sum_{j=1}^{p}v_{j}^{2})^{1/2}
the Euclidean norm and by
\|v\|_{\infty}=\max_{j\in[p]}|v_{j}|
the infinity norm. For a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, given subsets of indices
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[p]
and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq[q]
, denote
M_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}=(M_{ij})_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}
, and when
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
coincide, denote
M_{\mathcal{A}}=M_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}}
. We define
\mathbb{I}\{\cdot\}
as the indicator function, which takes the value of 1 if the condition inside
\{\cdot\}
is met and 0 otherwise. For ease of notation, we denote
\mathbb{I}_{{\mathcal{S}}}(i)=\mathbb{I}\{i\in{\mathcal{S}}\}
for a given index
i
and index set
{\mathcal{S}}
. For two positive sequences
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
, denote
x_{n}\lesssim y_{n}
if there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
n
such that
x_{n}\leq Cy_{n}
.

2
Anti-concentration inequalities

In this section, we establish new anti-concentration bounds. We begin with the case where the Gaussian vectors possess homogeneous component-wise variances. Then, we generalize the analysis to encompass the case with heterogeneous variances.

2.1
Anti-concentration under homogeneous component-wise variances

Our first theorem provides a dimension-free anti-concentration bound under the homogeneous variance setting.
We provide the proof of Theorem
2.1
in Section
5.1
(we note that the proof relies on Theorem
2.4
, whose proof is self-contained).

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{p})^{\top}
be a Gaussian random vector with mean
\mu=(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{p})^{\top}
and equal variances,
\operatorname{Var}(X_{i})=\sigma^{2}
for all
i\in[p]
. Let
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
be a nontrivial partition of
[p]
. Define
M_{\mathcal{A}}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}
and
M_{\mathcal{B}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}
.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|/\sigma\big),\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma\big)\!\!\Big\}\cdot\frac{7\varepsilon}{(1-\bar{\rho})\sigma},

(2.1)

where
\bar{\rho}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})
.

Remark 2.1
.

The bound in (
2.1
) indicates that the Lévy concentration scales with the smaller expected maximum of the two normalized Gaussian random vectors, and is invariant to shifting of the means.

In the presence of perfect positive correlations between the subsets, the following corollary characterizes the bounds of the Lévy concentration function with a stationary residual probability.

Corollary 2.2
.

Under the setting of Theorem
2.1
, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, we define the set

{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}=\{i\in\mathcal{A}:\exists j\in\mathcal{B}\text{ such that }\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})\geq 1-\delta\},

(2.2)

and for
\delta\in(0,1)
such that
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}\neq\mathcal{A}
, we define

\omega_{\delta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\exp\Big\{-\frac{1}{8\sigma^{2}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[\displaystyle\max_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{j}\big]\Big)_{+}^{2}\Big\},&\,\text{if \ ${\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}\neq\varnothing$,}\\
0,&\,\text{if \ ${\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}=\varnothing$.}\end{array}\right.

(2.3)

Suppose that
\{\delta\in(0,1):{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}\neq\mathcal{A}\}\neq\varnothing
.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)

(2.4)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\delta:{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}\neq\mathcal{A}}\!\Big\{\!\min\!\!\Big\{\mathbb{E}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}\!\!{|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|}/{\sigma}),\mathbb{E}(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\!{|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|}/{\sigma})\!\Big\}\!\cdot\!\frac{7\varepsilon}{\delta\sigma}+2\omega_{\delta}\!\Big\},

where the result also holds when
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are swapped.

Proof.

We provide the proof in Section
5.2
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

The condition
\{\delta\in(0,1):{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}\neq\mathcal{A}\}\neq\varnothing
rules out the case where all the random variables in partition
\mathcal{A}
are perfectly positively correlated with elements in
\mathcal{B}
. Essentially, the construction of the set
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}
with the threshold
\delta>0
removes the components with a correlation exceeding
1-\delta
with elements in the other subset, so that for the remaining components, the gap
1-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})
is at least
\delta
, and applying Theorem
2.1
yields a non-trivial bound. This gives the first term on the right-hand side (RHS) of (
2.4
), which provides a concentration bound. Meanwhile, the second term on the RHS of (
2.4
)
is a residual term, which
is the probability that the maximizer within set
\mathcal{A}
falls within the set
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}
.
It can be seen that the first concentration bound term decreases as
\delta
increases, while the second residual term increases with
\delta
, as a larger
\delta
enlarges the set
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}
.

The following proposition provides an illustrative example of the residual probability when the distribution of the Gaussian random vector is exchangeable. We provide the proof in Section
5.3
.

Proposition 2.3

(Lower Bound Case)
.

For some integers
1\leq k<m
and
p=2m-k
, let
(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\top}
be a Gaussian random vector with an equal mean
\mu_{0}
and equal variance
\sigma^{2}
, and suppose that
\bar{\rho}:=\max_{i,j\in[p],i\neq j}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
. Furthermore, assume that the distribution of the random vector is invariant with respect to any permutation of its components, i.e.,

\phi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})=\phi\big(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi({p})}\big),\quad\text{for all $(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ and $\pi\in\Pi^{[p]}$,}

(2.5)

where
\phi(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})
denotes the joint distribution of
(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\top}
at
(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})
, and
\Pi^{[p]}
denotes the set of all bijections from
[p]
to itself. Define the sets
\mathcal{A}=[m]
,
\mathcal{B}=[p]\backslash[m-k]

and let
M_{\mathcal{A}}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}
and
M_{\mathcal{B}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{k}{p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)

(2.6)

\displaystyle\leq\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}}\!{|X_{i}-\mu_{0}|}/{\sigma}\big),\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}}\!{|X_{j}-\mu_{0}|}/{\sigma}\big)\Big\}\cdot\!\frac{7\varepsilon}{(1-\bar{\rho})\sigma}+\frac{4k}{p+k}.

Moreover, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}:|t|>\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}\big(|M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}-t|\leq\varepsilon\big)

(2.7)

\displaystyle\leq\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}{|X_{i}-\mu_{0}|}/{\sigma}\big),\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}{|X_{j}-\mu_{0}|}/{\sigma}\big)\Big\}\cdot\frac{14\varepsilon}{(1-\bar{\rho})\sigma}.

Remark 2.3
.

By (
2.6
), we have that the Lévy concentration function is lower bounded by the ratio of the components with across-partition correlation equal to 1, and when the number of the perfectly positively correlated components
k
is sufficiently small relative to the number of components
p
, the residual probability is negligible compared with the concentration rate. Furthermore, the bound in (
2.7
) shows that the difference of the maxima still achieves the typical anti-concentration rate for
t
bounded away from 0.

2.2
Anti-concentration under heterogeneous variance conditions

Next, we provide anti-concentration results for Gaussian random vectors with heterogeneous variances.
Under some structural assumptions on the covariance matrix, we establish the following dimension-free bound for the Lévy concentration function.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\top}
be a Gaussian random vector with
\mathbb{E}(X_{i})=\mu_{i}
and

\operatorname{Var}(X_{i})=\sigma^{2}_{i}>0
for
i\in[p]
. For a nontrivial partition
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
of
[p]
, define
M_{\mathcal{A}}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}
and
M_{\mathcal{B}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}
.
Assume that
|{\rm Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})|<1
for any
i\in\mathcal{A}
and
j\in\mathcal{B}
, and one of the following two conditions is satisfied:

(A)

\max_{j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}\sigma_{j}^{-2}\sigma_{jj^{\prime}}\leq 1
, and
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}=\min_{j\in\mathcal{B},i\in\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{ij})>0
,

(B)

\max_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{i}^{-2}\sigma_{ii^{\prime}}\leq 1
, and
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}=\min_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{ij})>0
,

where
\sigma_{ij}=\mathbb{E}[(X_{j}-\mu_{j})(X_{i}-\mu_{i})]
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq 2\cdot\mathbb{E}\big({\max_{{\ell}\in{\mathcal{S}}}{|X_{\ell}-\mu_{\ell}|}/{\sigma_{\ell}}}\big)\frac{\varepsilon}{C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}},

(2.8)

where the set
{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{B}
when condition (A) is satisfied, and
{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{A}
when condition (B) is satisfied.

Proof.

Before proving Theorem
2.4
, we need the following lemma that characterizes the joint probability density function for
(M_{\mathcal{A}},M_{\mathcal{B}})
, and we provide the proof in Appendix
A.1
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\top}
be a Gaussian random vector with
\mathbb{E}(X_{i})=\mu_{i}
and

\operatorname{Var}(X_{i})=\sigma^{2}_{i}
for
i\in[p]
. For a nontrivial partition
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
of
[p]
, define
M_{\mathcal{A}}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}
and
M_{\mathcal{B}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}
.

(1) Suppose that
\sigma^{2}_{i}>0
for all
i\in[p]
,
{\rm Var}(X_{i}-X_{j})>0
for any
i\neq j
, and
|{\rm Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})|<1
for any
i\in\mathcal{A}
and
j\in\mathcal{B}
, then the joint distribution of
(M_{\mathcal{A}},M_{\mathcal{B}})
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and a version of its density is

f(x,y)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\{i\}}X_{i^{\prime}}\leq x,\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{j^{\prime}}\leq y\Big|X_{i}=x,X_{j}=y\Big)\phi_{i,j}(x,y),

(2.9)

where
\phi_{i,j}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the joint density function of
(X_{i},X_{j})
,
i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}
.

(2) If for all
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
and
i\neq j
, either
\operatorname{Var}(X_{i}-X_{j})>0
or
\mathbb{E}(X_{i}-X_{j})\neq 0
, and
\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
for all
i\in\mathcal{A}
,
then given any
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
F_{y}(x):=\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}\leq x,M_{\mathcal{B}}\leq y)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
, and a version of its partial derivative is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{y}(x)}{\partial x}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\{i\}}X_{i^{\prime}}\leq x,\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}\leq y\Big|X_{i}=x\Big)\phi_{i}(x),

(2.10)

where
\phi_{i}(\cdot)
is the marginal density function for
X_{i}
.

Note that when
\mathcal{B}=\varnothing
, the partial derivative in (
2.10
) reduces to the density function of
\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\in[p]}X_{i}\leq x)
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that condition (A) holds.
Also, without loss of generality, we assume that
{\rm Var}(X_{i}-X_{j})>0
for all
i\neq j
. Indeed, the inequality holds for
i\in\mathcal{A}
and
j\in\mathcal{B}
by the assumption that
|{\rm Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})|<1
for any
i\in\mathcal{A}
and
j\in\mathcal{B}
. In addition, for any
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
or
i,j\in\mathcal{B}
such that
i\neq j
, if
{\rm Var}(X_{i}-X_{j})=0
, there exits a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
X_{i}=X_{j}+c
almost surely. Then, we can remove either
X_{i}
or
X_{j}
based on the sign of
c
. We can repeat removing the components until
{\rm Var}(X_{i}-X_{j})>0
holds for all
i\neq j
.

Then by Lemma
2.5
, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\Big)=\int_{|y-x-t|\leq\varepsilon}f(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}=x,\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}=y)dxdy

\displaystyle=\int_{|y-x-t|\leq\varepsilon}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\!\!\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\{i\}}\!X_{i^{\prime}}\leq x,\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}\!X_{j^{\prime}}\leq y\Big|X_{i}=x,X_{j}=y\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\phi_{i,j}(x,y)\bigg\}dxdy

\displaystyle=\!\!\!\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t+\varepsilon}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\!\bigg\{\!\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\{i\}}X_{i^{\prime}}\leq x,\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{j^{\prime}}\leq x+u\Big|X_{i}=x,X_{j}-X_{i}=u\!\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\phi_{i,j}(x,x+u)|J|\bigg\}dxdu

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{t-\varepsilon}^{t+\varepsilon}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg\{\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}\!X_{i^{\prime}}\!\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x\Big|X_{i}=x,X_{j}\!-\!X_{i}=u\Big)

(2.11)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\phi_{i,j}(x,x+u)\bigg\}dxdu,

where
J
is the Jacobian matrix mapping
(x,u):=(x,y-x)\mapsto(x,y)
so that
|J|=1
. Note that we can write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x\Big|X_{i}=x,X_{j}-X_{i}=u\Big)\phi_{i,j}(x,x+u)dx

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\!\!\vee\!\!(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\!\leq x\Big|X_{i}\!=\!x,X_{j}\!=\!x+u\Big)\phi_{i|j}(x|x+u)\phi_{j}(x+u)dx,

where
\phi_{i|j}(\cdot|\cdot)
denotes the density of
X_{i}
conditional on
X_{j}
.
Then for any
j\in\mathcal{B}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

\displaystyle g_{u,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\!\vee\!(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\!\leq\!x\Big|X_{i}=x,X_{j}=x+u\Big)\phi_{i|j}(x|x+u);

(2.12)

\displaystyle H_{u,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x|X_{j}=x+u\right).

(2.13)

The following lemma establishes the relationship between
g_{u,j}(\cdot)
and
H_{u,j}(\cdot)

for a.e.
u\in\mathbb{R}
and every
j\in\mathcal{B}
(we provide its proof in Appendix
A.2
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
g_{u,j}(\cdot)
and
H_{u,j}(\cdot)
be the functions defined in (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) respectively. For every
j\in\mathcal{B}
and a.e.
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

g_{u,j}(x)=0,\quad\text{for all }x<x_{j}(u),

(2.14)

and

g_{u,j}(x)\leq\Big(1-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{j}^{-2}\sigma_{ij}\Big)^{-1}\frac{dH_{u,j}(x)}{dx},\quad\text{for all }x>x_{j}(u),

(2.15)

where
x_{j}(u)\in\mathbb{R}
is a predetermined value and might take the value of
-\infty
depending on the covariance structure (in which case (
2.14
) is trivial and (
2.15
) holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
).
2
2

2

The claim is nontrivial because
H_{u,j}(x)
is not a (conditional) cumulative function due to the tangling roles of
x
in both the quantile determination and the conditioning on
X_{j}
. Specifically, if we consider the function
G_{u,j}(x,y):=\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x|X_{j}=y+u\right)
, then
dH_{u,j}(x)/dx=\partial_{x}G_{u,j}(x,x)+\partial_{y}G_{u,j}(x,x)
. While the first component
\partial_{x}G_{u,j}(x,x)
gives rise to an upper bound of
g_{u,j}(x)
by applying Lemma
2.5
, the second component
\partial_{y}G_{u,j}(x,x)
highly depends on the covariance structure of
X
.

Applying Lemma
2.6
, since
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}>0
by condition (A),
we have that the following holds for a.e.
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x\Big|X_{i}=x,X_{j}-X_{i}=u\Big)\phi_{i,j}(x,x+u)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\phi_{j}(x+u)g_{u,j}(x)dx\overset{\rm(a)}{=}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}\phi_{j}(x+u)g_{u,j}(x)dx

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(b)}{\leq}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}\phi_{j}(x+u)\Big(1-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{j}^{-2}\sigma_{ij}\Big)^{-1}dH_{u,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\!\Big(1-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\sigma_{ij}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}\Big)^{-1}\!\!\!\int_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}\!\phi_{j}(x+u)d\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x|X_{j}=x+u\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(c)}{=}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\!\Big(1-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\frac{\sigma_{ij}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}\Big)^{-1}\Big\{\Big[\phi_{j}(x+u)\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x|X_{j}=x+u\Big)\Big]\Big|_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x|X_{j}=x+u\Big)d\phi_{j}(x+u)\Big\},

where equality (a) follows from (
2.14
), inequality (b) follows from (
2.15
), and equality (c) follows by integration by parts.
Denote by
{\rm RHS}_{(c)}
the term on the right hand side of equality (c), then we further have

\displaystyle{\rm RHS}_{(c)}

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(d)}{\leq}\!\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}\!\mathbb{P}(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\!\vee\!(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\!\leq x|X_{j}=x+u)\frac{x+u-\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}-\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{ij}}\phi_{j}(x+u)dx

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(e)}{\leq}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}(\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j^{\prime}}\leq x+u|X_{j}=x+u)\left|\frac{x+u-\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{ij}}\right|\phi_{j}(x+u)dx

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}(\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j^{\prime}}\leq x+u|X_{j}=x+u)\left|\frac{x+u-\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{ij}}\right|\phi_{j}(x+u)dx

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(f)}{\leq}C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathbb{P}(\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j^{\prime}}\leq\widetilde{x}|X_{j}=\widetilde{x})|\widetilde{x}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma_{j}\phi_{j}(\widetilde{x})d\widetilde{x},

where inequality (d) follows from the fact that

\Big[\phi_{j}(x+u)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq x|X_{j}=x+u)\Big]\Big|_{x_{j}(u)}^{+\infty}\leq 0,\quad\text{for all }j\in\mathcal{B},

inequality (e) holds as
\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)\leq\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A},j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}X_{i^{\prime}}\vee(X_{j^{\prime}}-u)
, and inequality (f) follows by taking
\widetilde{x}=x+u
and the definition of
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}=\min_{j\in\mathcal{B},i\in\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{ij})
. Furthermore, let
{\rm RHS}_{(f)}
be the term on the right hand side of inequality (f), and we have

\displaystyle{\rm RHS}_{(f)}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbb{I}\{\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{j^{\prime}}\leq X_{j}\}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma_{j}\,\Big|X_{j}=\widetilde{x}\Big)\phi_{j}(\widetilde{x})d\widetilde{x}

\displaystyle=C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{I}\{\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{j^{\prime}}\leq X_{j}\}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma_{j}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\Big\{\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\Big|\frac{X_{j}-\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{j}}\Big|\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{I}\{\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{j^{\prime}}<X_{j}\}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\Big|\frac{X_{j}-\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{j}}\Big|\sum_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{I}\{\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{j^{\prime}}=X_{j}\}\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(g)}{\leq}C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\Big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\Big|\frac{X_{j}-\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{j}}\Big|\Big),

where inequality (g) holds by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the fact that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\{j\}}X_{j^{\prime}}=X_{j}\Big)=0,\quad\text{for any $j\in\mathcal{B}$.}

Hence applying the above bound to the integral in (
2.11
), for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\Big)\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma_{j}\big)\frac{\varepsilon}{C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}},

and (
2.8
) follows by taking the supremum over
t
.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

The condition (A) or (B) of Theorem
2.4
is satisfied when one subset has a homogeneous component-wise variance that is greater than the component-wise variances of the other subset. For example, condition (A) is satisfied when we have
\sigma_{j}^{2}=1
for all
j\in\mathcal{B}
, and
\sigma_{i}^{2}\leq 1
for any
i\in\mathcal{A}
. In
[
9
]
, for the Gaussian random vector with heterogeneous marginal variances, an upper bound of the concentration function is obtained by scaling the random variables to have homogeneous component-wise variances, whereas in our case, the generalization of results from the equal variance case to the unequal variance case via scaling is nontrivial. For example, consider
p=1
, and let
M_{\mathcal{A}}=M_{\mathcal{B}}=X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
. Then the concentration behavior of
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}=X_{1}-X_{1}=0
and the scaled difference
M_{\mathcal{B}}-\frac{1}{2}M_{\mathcal{A}}=X_{1}-\frac{1}{2}X_{1}=\frac{1}{2}X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}/4)
will be fundamentally different. Specifically, while
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)=1
for any
\varepsilon>0
, the scaled difference has a well-bounded Lévy concentration function
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-\frac{1}{2}M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq\sqrt{8/\pi}\sigma^{-1}\varepsilon
. This indicates that for the case of the maximum difference, scaling can fundamentally change the Lévy concentration function and is not applicable for generalizing the results from the equal variance case to the unequal variance case.

The bound in (
2.8
) gives a dimension-free bound, similar to the case of the single Gaussian maximum
[
9
]
. Even though our approach is developed for the difference of maxima, it yields the following comparable bounds for the case of the single maximum. We provide the proof in Appendix
A.3
.

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})^{\top}
be a Gaussian random vector with
\mathbb{E}(X_{j})=\mu_{j}
and
\operatorname{Var}(X_{j})=\sigma_{j}^{2}>0
for
j\in[p]
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathcal{L}(\textstyle\max_{j\in[p]}X_{j},\varepsilon)\leq 2\mathbb{E}(\textstyle\max_{j\in[p]}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma_{j})\cdot\varepsilon/\underline{\sigma},

(2.16)

where
\underline{\sigma}=\min_{j\in[p]}\sigma_{j}
.

In particular, when
\operatorname{Var}(X_{j})=\sigma^{2}>0
for
j\in[p]
, (
2.16
) yields the upper bound
2\mathbb{E}[\max_{j\in[p]}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|/\sigma]\varepsilon/\sigma
for
\mathcal{L}(\textstyle\max_{j\in[p]}X_{j},\varepsilon)
, while
[
9
]
obtained the upper bound
4\mathbb{E}[\max_{j\in[p]}(X_{j}-\mu_{j})/\sigma+1]\varepsilon/\sigma
using a different proof.

By Corollary
2.7
, we can derive an alternative upper bound for the Lévy concentration function of the difference of two maxima of Gaussian random vectors in the next proposition.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
(X_{1},...,X_{p})^{\top}{=(X_{\mathcal{A}}^{\top},X_{\mathcal{B}}^{\top})}^{\top}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mu,\Sigma)
be a Gaussian random vector.
Also, let
(\widetilde{X}_{1},...,\widetilde{X}_{p})^{\top}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\widetilde{\Sigma})
be a Gaussian random vector that satisfies
(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathcal{A}}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}-\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}}
and
(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathcal{B}}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}}-\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}
.
Suppose
\min_{j\in[p]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}>0
, where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}^{2}=\operatorname{Var}(\widetilde{X}_{j})
for
j\in[p]
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq 2(\min_{j\in[p]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})^{-1}\min\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|\widetilde{X}_{i}|/\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}\big),\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|\widetilde{X}_{j}|/\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\big)\Big\}\cdot\varepsilon,

(2.17)

where
M_{\mathcal{A}}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}
and
M_{\mathcal{B}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}
.

Proof.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}-t|\leq\varepsilon)=\mathbb{E}\big(\mathbb{P}(|\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}-t|\leq\varepsilon\,\,|X_{\mathcal{A}})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\rm(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\big(\sup_{t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}(|\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-t^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon\,\,|X_{\mathcal{A}})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\rm(b)}{\leq}2\mathbb{E}\big(\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|\widetilde{X}_{j}|/\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\,\,\big|X_{\mathcal{A}}\big)\varepsilon/\min_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\rm(c)}{=}2\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|\widetilde{X}_{j}|/\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\big)\varepsilon/\min_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\leq 2(\min_{j\in[p]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|\widetilde{X}_{j}|/\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\big)\varepsilon,

where
(\widetilde{X}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{B}}=X_{\mathcal{B}}-\mu_{\mathcal{B}}-\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(X_{\mathcal{A}}-\mu_{\mathcal{A}})
is the residual vector that is independent of
X_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{E}(X_{\mathcal{A}})
. Inequality (a) follows from the fact that
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}
is deterministic conditional on
X_{\mathcal{A}}
, inequality (b) follows from Corollary
2.7
utilizing the conditional distribution of
X_{\mathcal{B}}
on
X_{\mathcal{A}}
, and equality (c) follows from the independence between
\widetilde{X}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
X_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Similarly, by conditioning on
X_{\mathcal{B}}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(|\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}-t|\leq\varepsilon)\leq 2(\min_{j\in[p]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j})^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}|\widetilde{X}_{j}|/\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}\big)\varepsilon,

where
(\widetilde{X}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{A}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}-\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}})
.
Hence combining the above results, we have that (
2.17
) holds.
∎

Proposition
2.8
is a refinement of Lemma 5 in
[
21
]
, which shows

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{A}\subsetneq[p]\\
\mathcal{B}=[p]\backslash\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq\frac{2\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\underline{\lambda}}}(\sqrt{2\log p}+2),\quad\underline{\lambda}:=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{A}\subsetneq[p]\\
\mathcal{B}=[p]\backslash\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\min_{j\in\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}-\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}})_{jj},

and they lower bound
\underline{\lambda}
by
\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma)
for positive definite covariance matrices.
While Lemma 5 in
[
21
]
aims to obtain uniform bound over all
\mathcal{A}\subsetneq[p]
, (
2.17
) is more adaptive to specific subsets and allows for possibly sharper rates when
|\mathcal{A}|\ll|\mathcal{B}|
, e.g., when
|\mathcal{A}|=O(1)
, the bound in (
2.17
) scales with a constant instead of
\sqrt{\log p}
as in
[
21
]
.

Remark 2.5
.

We compare the bound in (
2.17
) with the bound in (
2.8
). The most favorable scenario for (
2.17
) occurs when
p=2
,
X=(X_{1},X_{2})^{\top}
,
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=1
and
\Sigma_{12}=\rho<1
. Then the density of
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
is upper bounded by
(1/\sqrt{4\pi})(1-\rho)^{-1/2}
. In comparison, the upper bound in (
2.8
) gives
\sqrt{8/\pi}(1-\rho)^{-1}\varepsilon
, while the upper bound in (
2.17
) yields
\sqrt{8/\pi}\sqrt{(1-\rho)(1+\rho)}\varepsilon
. Hence, it can be seen that relative to the true density, our bound in (
2.8
) is conservative by a factor of
O(\sqrt{1-\rho})
, whereas the bound in (
2.17
) carries an excess factor of
\sqrt{1+\rho}
. Thus, in non-degenerate scenarios, our bound in (
2.8
) is tighter when
\rho<0
, and the bound in (
2.17
) is tighter when
\rho>0
. It remains an open problem whether it is possible to close the gap of the extra factor
\sqrt{1-\bar{\rho}}
when
p>2
. We further investigate the tightness of the bound with respect to
1-\bar{\rho}
for
p>2
through numerical studies in Appendix
D
. However, it is clear that
\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma)
does not prevent (
2.8
) from delivering quite useful bounds.

3
Application: Central limit theorems for the distribution of the maximizers of empirical processes

Here, we establish the Gaussian approximation for the distribution of the maximizers of empirical processes indexed by a discrete set. Earlier works mainly focus on establishing Gaussian approximations for the supremum of empirical processes
[
7
,
10
,
15
]
. However, in some recent applications, such as combinatorial inferences
[
31
,
38
,
29
,
21
]
, the main interest lies in the maximizers of some empirical processes. Therefore, by casting the maximizing event as the difference of two maxima,
we integrate our new anti-concentration bounds with existing Gaussian approximation techniques to establish novel central limit theorems for this application. In particular, our central limit theorems cover the setting where the covariance matrix is singular, such as when
X_{\mathcal{A}}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}Z
and
X_{\mathcal{B}}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}Z
, where
Z
is a
d
-dimensional Gaussian random vector and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}},\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
with
p>d
.

Specifically, let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be independent random vectors with the same mean
\mu=\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i})
and possibly different covariance matrices
\Sigma_{i}=\mathbb{E}\big[(\xi_{i}-\mu)(\xi_{i}-\mu)^{\top}\big]
for
i\in[n]
.
Meanwhile, let
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be independent Gaussian random vectors with
\eta_{i}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mu,\Sigma_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. We define

X=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\eta_{i}-\mu+a),\quad Y=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\xi_{i}-\mu+a),

where
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a known deterministic vector, and denote by
\Sigma^{*}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma_{i}
with
\sigma_{j}^{2}=\Sigma^{*}_{jj}
for
j\in[p]
. Here we add the shifting vector
a
to allow for more general non-centered distributions, such as when location information is incorporated to perform multiple testing of moment inequalities as in
[
1
]
.
Let
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
be a nontrivial partition of
[p]
. Next, we let

M_{\mathcal{A}}^{X}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}X_{j},\quad M_{\mathcal{B}}^{X}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j},\quad M_{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}Y_{j},\quad M_{\mathcal{B}}^{Y}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}Y_{j}.

Our objective is to estimate the probability that the maximum component of
Y
lies within the set
\mathcal{A}
with the probability that the maximum component of

X
lies within the same set
\mathcal{A}
. To do that, we need to control:

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}Y_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A})-\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}X_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A})\right|

(3.1)

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{Y}>0)-\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{X}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{X}>0)\right|,

which connects with the probability of the difference of the maxima being positive.
Further, as the mean and the covariance matrix of
X
are usually unknown in practice, we construct the multiplier bootstrap random vector to approximate
X
:

\widehat{X}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[e_{i}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi})+a],

(3.2)

where
\bar{\xi}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}
and
e_{i}\overset{\rm i.i.d.}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
are independent of
\xi_{1:n}:=\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Then we will control

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}Y_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A})-\mathbb{P}(\textstyle\mathop{\text{\rm arg\,max}}_{j\in[p]}\widehat{X}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A}|\xi_{1:n})\right|

(3.3)

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{Y}>0)-\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{\widehat{X}}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{\widehat{X}}>0)\right|,

where
M_{\mathcal{A}}^{\widehat{X}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{X}_{j}
and
M_{\mathcal{B}}^{\widehat{X}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\widehat{X}_{j}
. Without loss of generality, by the constructions of
X
,
Y
and
\widehat{X}
, we assume that
\mu=\mathbf{0}
.

The application of the anti-concentration theorems in Section
2
is essential for bounding (
3.1
) and (
3.3
). To facilitate our discussion, we introduce some conditions on the covariance structure.

Condition 3.1

(Covariance condition)
.

Let
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a random vector with covariance matrix
\Sigma
and let
\sigma_{j}^{2}=\Sigma_{jj}
for
j=1,\ldots,p
. We say
Z
satisfies the covariance condition with respect to the nontrivial partition
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
of
[p]
if
one of the following conditions holds,

(A)

\max_{j,j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}\sigma_{j}^{-2}\Sigma_{jj^{\prime}}\leq 1
, and
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:=\min_{j\in\mathcal{B},i\in\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{j}^{-1}\Sigma_{ij})>0
,

(B)

\max_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{i}^{-2}\Sigma_{ii^{\prime}}\leq 1
, and
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}:=\min_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{i}^{-1}\Sigma_{ij})>0
,

and that
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}|\operatorname{Corr}(Z_{i},Z_{j})|<1
when there exists
i,j\in[p]
such that
\sigma_{i}^{2}\neq\sigma_{j}^{2}
.

Next, we bound (
3.1
) and (
3.3
) in the following theorem, and we provide the proof in Section
5.4
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be independent random vectors with
\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i})=\mu
and
\Sigma_{i}=\mathbb{E}\big[(\xi_{i}-\mu)(\xi_{i}-\mu)^{\top}\big]
for
i\in[n]
, and
let
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be independent Gaussian random vectors with
\eta_{i}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mu,\Sigma_{i})
for
i\in[n]
. Define
X=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\eta_{i}-\mu+a)
and
Y=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\xi_{i}-\mu+a)
.
Assume
\Sigma^{*}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma_{i}
satisfies the covariance Condition
3.1
for a nontrivial partition
\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
of
[p]
, and suppose that there exist a
B_{n}\geq 1
and a constant
b_{0}
such that we have

\mathbb{E}\big(\exp(|\xi_{ij}|/B_{n})\big)\leq 2,\quad\text{for all }i\in[n],j\in[p],

(3.4)

and

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\xi_{ij}^{4})\leq B_{n}^{2}b_{0}^{2},\quad\text{for all }j\in[p].

(3.5)

Furthermore, assume that
B_{n}^{2}\log^{5}(pn)\leq cn
for a small enough constant
c>0
.
Then, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
b_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{Y}\leq s)-\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{X}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{X}\leq s)\right|

(3.6)

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm p}}|X_{\ell}-\mathbb{E}(X_{\ell})|/\sigma_{\ell}\big)}{C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}\left(\frac{B_{n}^{2}\log^{3}(pn)}{n}\right)^{1/4},

where
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm p}=\mathcal{B}
when (A) of Condition
3.1
is satisfied, and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm p}=\mathcal{A}
when (B) of Condition
3.1
is satisfied. Meanwhile, for the multiplier bootstrap random vector
\widehat{X}
as defined in (
3.2
), with probability at least
1-1/(2n^{4})-1/n-3({B_{n}^{2}\log^{3}(pn)/n})^{1/2}
, we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{Y}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{Y}\leq s)-\mathbb{P}(M_{\mathcal{A}}^{\widehat{X}}-M_{\mathcal{B}}^{\widehat{X}}\leq s\,|\,\xi_{1:n})\right|

(3.7)

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}[\max_{\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\rm p}}|X_{\ell}-\mathbb{E}(X_{\ell})|/\sigma_{\ell}]}{C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}}\left(\frac{B_{n}^{2}\log^{3}(pn)}{n}\right)^{1/4}.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
follows the iterative randomized Lindeberg technique from
[
12
]
with an application of the anti-concentration bounds developed in the previous section. Moreover, it smooths the function as follows:

\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R},v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left|\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(Y_{n})-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(Y_{n})\leq s\Big)-\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(X_{n})-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(X_{n})\leq s\Big)\right|,

where
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
are two
p
-dimensional random vectors with distributions depend on
n
, and
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{v}(x)=\max_{j\in{\mathcal{S}}}(x_{j}+v_{j})
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
{\mathcal{S}}\in\{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\}
.
To bound this, we first construct a smooth approximation function
\varphi_{s}^{v}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for the indicator function of the difference of the two maxima such that for all
(x,v,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(x)-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(x)\leq s-\delta\}\leq\varphi_{s}^{v}(x)\leq\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(x)-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(x)\leq s+2\delta\},

where
\delta>0
is a tuning parameter controlling the accuracy of approximation. Then, by the triangle inequality, we bound the distance by

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R},v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left|\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(Y_{n})-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(Y_{n})\leq s\Big)-\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(X_{n})-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(X_{n})\leq s\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R},v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left|\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(X_{n})-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(X_{n})\leq s\Big)-\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}^{v}(X_{n})-\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}^{v}(X_{n})\leq s+3\delta\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{p},s\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{s}^{v}(Y_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[\varphi_{s}^{v}(X_{n})]\right|.

where we bound the first term by applying the anti-concentration bounds developed in Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.4
, and we bound the second term on the RHS by the iterative randomized Lindeberg method in
[
12
]
.

Remark 3.1
.

The smoothing and the anti-concentration results developed in the previous section can be applied to achieve more general bootstrap approximation results following the arguments in
[
12
]
. We refer interested readers to
[
12
]
for details.

4
Numerical experiments

We conduct thorough numerical experiments under several different settings to empirically benchmark the anti-concentration bounds developed earlier. We also compare our bounds with
[
21
]
empirically, where the authors derive an anti-concentration bound by conditioning on one of the two Gaussian random vectors, with the Lévy concentration function scaling with the minimal eigenvalue of the covariance matrix.

Define
\mathcal{A}=\{1,\ldots,p/2\}
and
\mathcal{B}=\{p/2+1,\ldots,p\}
. Denote by
X_{\mathcal{A}}
and
X_{\mathcal{B}}
the Gaussian random vectors corresponding to the sets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively. We generate
X_{\mathcal{A}}
and
X_{\mathcal{B}}
by

X_{\mathcal{A}}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}Z+\mu_{\mathcal{A}},\text{ and }X_{\mathcal{B}}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}Z+\mu_{\mathcal{B}},\quad X_{\mathcal{A}},X_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p/2},

(4.1)

where
Z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d})
is a
d
-dimensional standard Gaussian random vector, and we pre-specify
\mu_{\mathcal{A}},\mu_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p/2}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}},\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p/2\times d}
for different settings. Letting
M_{\mathcal{A}}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}X_{i}
and
M_{\mathcal{B}}=\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}
, we evaluate the Lévy concentration function
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
under different covariance structures.

We repeat the generation scheme 5,000 times for each setting and report the empirical performance. Specifically, to evaluate
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
, we present the empirical distribution of
\mathbb{P}(|M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}-t|\leq\varepsilon)
over a range of
t
, where we compute the empirical probabilities over 1,000 equidistant points of
t
from the minimum to the maximum realizations of
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
and present the maximum empirical probability. Next, to evaluate the dependence of
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
on
p
, we plot the normalized interval length
\sqrt{\log p}\varepsilon/\widehat{\sigma}
versus

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
, where
\widehat{\sigma}=p^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sigma_{i}
is the average standard deviation for
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
. In Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, we consider the cases where
d<p
, and in Section
4.3
, we compare our bounds with that in
[
21
]
under the full rank setting.

4.1
Homogeneous variance case

We begin by discussing the homogeneous variance case. The entries of the matrices
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
are drawn independently from a standard Gaussian distribution. To ensure homogeneous variance across ultivariate Gaussian random vectors
X_{\mathcal{A}}
and
X_{\mathcal{B}}
, we normalize the row norms of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
to unity. We consider dimensions
p\in\{2000,3000,4000,5000\}
, and change the number of overlapping rows between
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
to assess the impact of violating the condition
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
on the Lévy concentration function.

We first consider
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
when
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
. We present the empirical results in Figure
1
. In particular, for the centered case that
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
with
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}=\mu_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbf{0}
,
Figure
1
(a) shows that
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
scales linearly with
\sqrt{\log p}\varepsilon/\widehat{\sigma}
, and
Figure
1
(b) provides the empirical densities of normalized
M_{\mathcal{A}}-M_{\mathcal{B}}
. We then consider the case of non-centered
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
, where we draw each
\mu_{i}
independently from an exponential distribution with rate 1. We provide the results in Figures
1
(c) and (d), and we observe that the results are similar to the centered case. This is consistent with our theoretical findings.

Then, we consider the extreme case where
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=1
with centered
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
, and we provide the empirical results in Figure
2
. Figures
2
(a) and (b) consider the case where the number of overlapping rows between
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
is
K=p/8
, and Figures
2
(c) and (d)
consider the case of varying
K\in\{100,200,300,400\}
with
p=4000
.
By the density plots in Figures
2
(b) and (d), we observe that the distributions of
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
concentrate towards zero. Also, by
Figure
2
(c), we find that the intercepts of the lines scale approximately in proportion with
K
.

To verify whether
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
scales with the lesser of
\mathbb{E}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|)
and
\mathbb{E}(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|)
, as indicated by Theorem
2.1
when
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are of different sizes, we conduct an additional experiment. In this experiment, we define
\mathcal{A}=\{1,\ldots,k_{0}\}
and
\mathcal{B}=\{k_{0}+1,\ldots,p\}
with
\mu_{i}=0
for all
i\in[p]
, and examine how
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},0.05)
scales with
p
when
k_{0}
is fixed at {100, 200, 300, 400}. We present the results in Figure
3
. We can see that the Lévy concentration function plateaus after
p\geq 2k_{0}
, i.e.,
|\mathcal{B}|\geq|\mathcal{A}|
. This observation is consistent with Theorem
2.1
.

(a)
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
when
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}=\mu_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbf{0}

(b) Density curves when
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}=\mu_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbf{0}

​​​​

​​​​​

(c)
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
when
\mu_{\mathcal{A}},\mu_{\mathcal{B}}\neq\mathbf{0}

(d) Density curves when
\mu_{\mathcal{A}},\mu_{\mathcal{B}}\neq\mathbf{0}

​​​​

​​​​​

Figure 1
:
Anti-concentration results of
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
for the homogeneous variance case with
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
. Panels (a) and (c) show that the empirical evaluation of the Lévy concentration function scales linearly with
\varepsilon
under both the centered and uncentered cases. Panels (b) and (d) present the density curves, where
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
is normalized by the sample mean and standard deviation for comparability. Empirically, we have
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=\{0.916,0.913,0.923,0.924\}
for
p=\{2000,3000,4000,5000\}
respectively.

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
with varied
p

(b) Density curves with varied
p

​​​​

\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
with varied
K

(d) Density curves with varied
K

​​​​

​​​​​

Figure 2
:
Anti-concentration results of
M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}
for the homogeneous variance case with
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=1
. Panels (a) and (b) evaluate the anti-concentration behaviors at different
p
when the number of overlapping components between
X_{\mathcal{A}}
and
X_{\mathcal{B}}
is
K=p/8
; Panels (c) and (d) evaluate the anti-concentration behaviors for varying
K
at
p=4000
.

Figure 3
:
Scaling of
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},0.05)
with
p
when
\mathcal{A}=[k_{0}]
and
\mathcal{B}=[p]\backslash[k_{0}]
at
k_{0}\in\{100,200,300,400\}
for centered
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
with unit component-wise variances. The empirical evaluation of
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},0.05)
plateaus after
p\geq 2k_{0}
, i.e.,
|\mathcal{B}|\geq|\mathcal{A}|
. This suggests that the Lévy concentration function scales with the lesser of
\mathbb{E}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|)
and
\mathbb{E}(\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{j}-\mu_{j}|)
when
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are of different sizes, which is consistent with Theorem
2.1
.

4.2
Heterogeneous variance case

We evaluate the anti-concentration behaviors under the heterogeneous variance setting with the covariance conditions on
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
in Theorem
2.4
satisfied and violated, respectively, i.e.,
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}>0
and
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}\leq 0
. We take
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}=\mu_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbf{0}
in this subsection.

We first consider the case where the covariance condition is satisfied that
\max_{i,j\in\mathcal{B}}\sigma_{i}^{-2}\sigma_{ij}\leq 1
, and
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}=\min_{j\in\mathcal{B},i\in\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{ij})>0
. In particular, we draw the entries of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
independently from the standard Gaussian distribution. We then normalize the row norms of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
to 1 and scale
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
by the inverse of its maximal row norm. In this case,
\sigma_{j}^{2}=1
for
j\in\mathcal{B}
and
\sigma_{i}^{2}\leq 1
for
i\in\mathcal{A}
. We present the results in Figure
4
. In the design illustrated by Figure
4
, we have that
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)
scales linearly with
\sqrt{\log p}\varepsilon/\widehat{\sigma}
when the covariance condition is satisfied.

Next, we consider the case in which the covariance condition is violated. That is, when
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
, and
\max\{\min_{j\in\mathcal{B},i\in\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{ij}),\min_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}(\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{ij})\}\leq 0
. Specifically, we let
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}=\{i\in\mathcal{A}:\sigma_{i}-\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{ij}\leq 0\}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}=\{j\in\mathcal{B}:\sigma_{j}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{ij}\leq 0\}
be the sets of indices violating the condition of
C_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}>0
in sets
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively, and let
\nu_{\mathcal{A}}=|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}|/|\mathcal{A}|
and
\nu_{\mathcal{B}}=|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}|/|\mathcal{B}|
be the corresponding violation ratios.
We then let
m_{\mathcal{A}}=|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}}(\sigma_{i}-\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}\sigma_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{ij})
and
m_{\mathcal{B}}=|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}|^{-1}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}}(\sigma_{j}-\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{ij})
. We consider the full rank and equal correlation setting by letting
\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=0.9
for all
i\neq j
. Then, we consider different
\{\sigma_{j}\}_{j\in[p]}
by scaling the row norms of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
for different violation ratios

\nu_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\nu_{\mathcal{B}}
.

We present the empirical results in Figure
5
for different settings. In Figures
5
(a) and (b), we set
\sigma_{j}^{2}=1
for all
j\in[p]
as the benchmark. In Figures
5
(c) and (d), we set

(\sigma^{2}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{A}}=(\sigma^{2}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{B}}=(0.9\cdot\mathbf{1}_{p/4}^{\top},\,\mathbf{1}_{p/8}^{\top},\,10\cdot\mathbf{1}_{p/8}^{\top})^{\top},

and we have
\nu_{\mathcal{A}}=\nu_{\mathcal{B}}=0.75
and
m_{\mathcal{A}}=m_{\mathcal{B}}=-8.067
. In Figures
5
(e) and (f), we set

(\sigma^{2}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{A}}=(\sigma^{2}_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{B}}=(0.9\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3p/8}^{\top},\,\mathbf{1}_{p/16}^{\top},\,15\cdot\mathbf{1}_{p/16}^{\top})^{\top},

and we have
\nu_{\mathcal{A}}=\nu_{\mathcal{B}}=0.875
and
m_{\mathcal{A}}=m_{\mathcal{B}}=-12.586
. By Figures
5
(b), (d), and (f), as the violation ratios
\nu_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\nu_{\mathcal{B}}
increase, we observe a higher concentration. However, this may be due to the reduction in the smallest marginal variance
\min_{j\in[p]}\sigma_{j}^{2}
after the normalization of
M_{\mathcal{A}}-M_{\mathcal{B}}
. Meanwhile, Figures
5
(a), (c), and (e) show that the Lévy concentration function still scales linearly with
\sqrt{\log p}\varepsilon/\widehat{\sigma}
.

Finally, we consider the case where
\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=1
with centered
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
, and the perfectly correlated random variables from different subsets have unequal variances. In this setup, we set
K=p/8
and scale the norm of the overlapping rows in
\Gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathcal{B}}
to 1 and 1/2, respectively. We provide empirical results in Figures
6
(a) and (b). In this case, we do not observe the concentrations in the empirical density plot as in the homogeneous variance cases.

(a)
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)

(b) Density curves

4.3
Empirical comparison of the theoretical bounds with literature

We compare the ratio of
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)/\varepsilon
with the theoretical bounds in Theorem
2.4
, Proposition
2.8
and Lemma 5 of
[
21
]
. As discussed earlier in Section
1
, there are examples where the previous bounds in literature are not informative. Here we consider designs that highlight the difference even in well-behaved cases where
the covariance matrix of
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
is non-degenerate. We let
\Sigma^{0}=\Gamma\Gamma^{\top}+\mathbf{I}_{p}
, where
\Gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times\frac{p}{10}}
is a matrix with entries independently sampled from standard Gaussian distribution. We take
\Sigma={\rm diag}(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}\Sigma^{0}{\rm diag}(\Sigma^{0})^{-1/2}
as the covariance matrix for
(X_{\mathcal{A}}^{\top},X_{\mathcal{B}}^{\top})^{\top}
, and let
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}=\mu_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbf{0}
. We consider
p\in\{2000,3000,4000,5000\}
. For each setting, we compute the theoretical bound for the ratio by Theorem
2.4
as
2\cdot\max\big\{\mathbb{E}[\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}|],\mathbb{E}[\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{j}|]\big\}/(1-\bar{\rho})
, where

\bar{\rho}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})
. The theoretical bound by Proposition
2.8
is
2\cdot\max\big\{\mathbb{E}[\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|\widetilde{X}_{i}/\widetilde{\sigma}_{i}|],\mathbb{E}[\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}|\widetilde{X}_{j}/\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}|]\big\}/\min_{j\in[p]}\widetilde{\sigma}_{j}
, where
\widetilde{X}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\widetilde{\Sigma})
with
\widetilde{\Sigma}={\rm diag}(\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}-\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}},\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}}-\Sigma_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{A}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}\Sigma_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}})
and
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}_{j}=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{jj}
for
j\in[p]
. We compare the bounds by Theorem
2.4
and Proposition
2.8
with the bound of Lemma 5 in
[
21
]
,
2(\sqrt{2\log p}+2)/\sigma_{\min}
, where
\sigma_{\min}^{2}=\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{X})
. Table
1
shows the results at
\varepsilon=0.05
. Theorem
2.4
provides a tighter bound by bounding the concentration function based only on pair-wise correlations instead of relying on the minimal eigenvalue of the covariance matrix.

:

[

]
against empirical ratio of
\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)/\varepsilon
at
\varepsilon=0.05
.

5
Proofs of main theoretical results

We provide the technical proofs for the main results in Sections
2
and
3
.

5.1
Proof of Theorem
2.1

Note that when
\bar{\rho}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=1
, the right-hand side of (
2.1
) goes to infinity. Therefore, we only prove the results when
\bar{\rho}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
. Without loss of generality, suppose that there is no perfect positive correlation within
\mathcal{A}
or
\mathcal{B}
that
\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
for any
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
or
i,j\in\mathcal{B}
and
i\neq j
. If there exists a pair
(X_{i},X_{j})
in the same set that is perfectly positively correlated, because
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{j}=\sigma
, there exists a constant
c
such that
X_{i}\equiv X_{j}+c
, and we can remove
X_{i}
or
X_{j}
according to the sign of
c
without changing the value of
M_{\mathcal{A}}
or
M_{\mathcal{B}}
. Denote the sets of indices with perfect negative correlation with another variable in the other set as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\{i\in\mathcal{A}:\exists j\in\mathcal{B}\text{ such that }\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=-1\},

(5.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\{i\in\mathcal{B}:\exists j\in\mathcal{A}\text{ such that }\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=-1\}.

If
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}
are empty, the result directly follows from Theorem
2.4
. Hence we suppose that
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}
are nonempty. Lemma
B.3
establishes that there is a one-to-one mapping between
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}
, and the Gaussian random variables within each set are independent.
For ease of presentation, we assume that
\mathcal{A}\neq{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{B}\neq{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}
(the results when either
\mathcal{A}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}
or
\mathcal{B}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}
follow by similar arguments). We define the events

\mathcal{E}_{1}=\{\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}X_{i}\geq\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}X_{i^{\prime}}\},\quad\mathcal{E}_{2}=\{\max_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}}X_{j}\geq\max_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}}X_{j^{\prime}}\}.

Then for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\!\big|M_{\mathcal{B}}\!-\!M_{\mathcal{A}}\!-\!t\big|\!\leq\!\varepsilon\!\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\Big(\!\!\{\big|M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\}\!\cap\!\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\Big)}_{\rm I_{1}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\Big(\!\!\{\big|M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\}\!\cap\!\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}\Big)}_{\rm I_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\Big(\{\big|M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\}\cap\{\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\}\Big)}_{\rm I_{3}}.

We then bound the terms
{\rm I_{1}}
,
{\rm I_{2}}
and
{\rm I_{3}}
. For
{\rm I_{1}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm I_{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\{\big|\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}X_{i}-\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\big\}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}X_{i}-\max_{j\in\mathcal{B}}X_{j}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\Big).

By the definition of
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}
in (
5.1
), for any
i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
j\in\mathcal{B}
, it holds that
|\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})|<1
. Hence conditions (A) and (B) of Theorem
2.4
are both satisfied since
\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{j}^{-1}\sigma_{ij}=\sigma-\sigma\cdot\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})>0
for any
i,j\in[p]
and
i\neq j
. Then applying Theorem
2.4
, we have that

\displaystyle{\rm I}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\wedge\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\Big\}\frac{\varepsilon}{(1-\bar{\rho}_{1})\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\wedge\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\Big\}\frac{\varepsilon}{(1-\bar{\rho})\sigma^{2}},

where

\bar{\rho}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})
and
\bar{\rho}_{1}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})\leq\bar{\rho}
. Following similar arguments, we have

{\rm I}_{2}\leq 2\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\wedge\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\Big\}\frac{\varepsilon}{(1-\bar{\rho})\sigma^{2}}.

For the term
{\rm I_{3}}
, applying Lemma
B.2
, we have that

\displaystyle{\rm I}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(\!\!\big\{\big|\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}X_{i}-\max_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}}X_{j}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\big\}\!\cap\!\{\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\}\!\!\Big)\overset{\rm(a)}{\leq}\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}X_{i}-\max_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}}X_{j}-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(b)}{=}\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}X_{i}-\max_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}(-X_{i}+c_{i})-t\big|\leq\varepsilon\big)\overset{\rm(c)}{\leq}3\mathbb{E}\Big(\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\Big)\frac{\varepsilon}{(1+\underline{\rho}_{3})\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{\rm(d)}{\leq}3\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\wedge\mathbb{E}\big(\max_{i\in\mathcal{B}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|\big)\Big\}\frac{\varepsilon}{(1-\bar{\rho})\sigma^{2}},

where inequality (a) follows by dropping the intersection, equality (b) holds by Lemma
B.3
, inequality (c) follows from Lemma
B.2
, and inequality (d) follows from
\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|=\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}}|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|
due to the equivalence in (
B.4
) of Lemma
B.3
, and that

\underline{\rho}_{3}\!:=\min_{i,j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})=\min_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}},j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}}(-\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})\!)=-\!\!\!\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{A}},j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\!\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})\geq-\bar{\rho}.

Combining the three bounds above, we have that (
2.1
) holds.

5.2
Proof of Corollary
2.2

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1
for any
i,j\in\mathcal{A}
or
i,j\in\mathcal{B}
and
i\neq j
.
To prove the claim (
2.4
), it suffices to show that, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
such that the corresponding
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}
defined in (
2.2
) satisfies
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}\neq\mathcal{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon)\leq\left\{\mathbb{E}\!\left(\!\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}\!\!\frac{|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|}{\sigma}\right)\!\wedge\!\mathbb{E}\!\left(\!\max_{i\in\mathcal{B}}\!\frac{|X_{i}-\mu_{i}|}{\sigma}\right)\right\}\cdot\!\frac{7\varepsilon}{\delta\sigma}+2\omega_{\delta}.

(5.2)

First, we consider the case
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}=\varnothing
. Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have

\bar{\rho}=\textstyle\max_{i\in\mathcal{A},j\in\mathcal{B}}\operatorname{Corr}(X_{i},X_{j})<1-\delta,

and the assumptions of Theorem
2.1
are satisfied. Applying Theorem
2.1
, together with the fact
\bar{\rho}<1-\delta
, we have that (
5.2
) holds as desired.

Next, consider the case
{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}\neq\varnothing
and
D_{\delta}=\mathbb{E}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i})-\mathbb{E}(\max_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{j})\leq 0
. We have that
\omega_{\delta}=1
by its definition in (
2.3
), and (
5.2
) holds since its right-hand side is larger than 1.

Next, consider the case of
\delta\in(0,1)
such that
D_{\delta}=\mathbb{E}(\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i})-\mathbb{E}(\max_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{j})>0
. We first define the event

\mathcal{E}=\Big\{\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}\geq\max_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i^{\prime}}\Big\}.

For any subset
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq[p]
, let
X_{{\mathcal{S}}}=(X_{i})_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}
be the subvector corresponding to
{\mathcal{S}}
. Note that we can write
X_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2}Z_{{\mathcal{S}}}+\mu_{{\mathcal{S}}}
, where
\mu_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\mathbb{E}X_{{\mathcal{S}}}
,
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\mathbb{E}[(X_{{\mathcal{S}}}-\mu_{{\mathcal{S}}})(X_{{\mathcal{S}}}-\mu_{{\mathcal{S}}})^{\top}]
,
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2}
is a symmetric matrix that satisfies
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\big(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2}\big)^{2}
,
Z_{{\mathcal{S}}}=\big(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2}\big)^{-1}(X_{{\mathcal{S}}}-\mu_{{\mathcal{S}}})
, and the components of
Z_{{\mathcal{S}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
are independent standard Gaussian random variables. Further, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we have that

\left|\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2}x+\mu_{{\mathcal{S}}})_{i}-\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2}y+\mu_{{\mathcal{S}}})_{i}\right|\leq\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}\|(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2})_{i}\|_{2}\|x-y\|_{2}=\sigma\|x-y\|_{2},

where
(\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2})_{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{1/2}
. Hence we know that both
\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}X_{i}
and
-\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}X_{i}
are
\sigma
-Lipschitz with respect to
Z_{{\mathcal{S}}}
. By the log-Sobolev inequality (see Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C.6
), for any
r>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}X_{i}-\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}X_{i}\geq r\big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{r^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Big),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}X_{i}-\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}}X_{i}\leq-r)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{r^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Big).

Hence, for
D_{\delta}>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{c})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{\left\{\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}\leq\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}+\frac{D_{\delta}}{2}\right\}\cap\left\{\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}\geq\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}-\frac{D_{\delta}}{2}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}\geq\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}+\frac{D_{\delta}}{2}\right\}-\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}\leq\mathbb{E}\max_{i\in\mathcal{A}\backslash{\mathcal{N}}_{\delta}}X_{i}-\frac{D_{\delta}}{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq 1-2\exp\left(-\frac{D_{\delta}^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right)=1-2\omega_{\delta},

where the last inequality holds by (
5.3
).
Then, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|M_{\mathcal{B}}-M_{\mathcal{A}}-t|\leq\varepsilon)
